Doug
17:29 UTC, 18 September 2026
Interesting task. Let's invent a react library that grows from representing simple writing to evolving into representing whole books...
Start with me at the text level. All it does it take text. Nothing else is allowed.
<Text>Whatever as long as its not a component in this library.</Text>
You can basically write all react in there as long as it is not recursive text. Following so far? I want you to get the idea not expand yet
Claude
17:29 UTC, 18 September 2026
Following. <Text> is the leaf of the library's grammar. It's the one node that closes a branch.
The split you're drawing is between structure and content. Library components are structure — they're the thing the library actually models. Everything else is content: arbitrary React, spans, emphasis, someone's custom <Tooltip>, whatever. That's allowed inside <Text> because it's opaque to the model. It renders, but it doesn't mean anything structurally.
So the document model is exactly the tree of library components, and <Text> is the frontier where that model stops and plain rendering takes over. No recursion at the leaf means the tree always terminates in something the library can treat as a flat unit.
One thing I'd want to know before anything gets built on top: whether the constraint is lexical (nothing from the library appears in that JSX) or semantic (nothing from the library ends up in the rendered subtree). A foreign component that internally renders a library component would satisfy the first and violate the second.
Ready for the next level whenever you are.
Doug
17:34 UTC, 18 September 2026
Text then specifies. He evolves into Writing. Writing is specified to only take other types of writing. Text is a type of writing. But he overrides the specification to allow his original specification, making him a sort of null writing
<Writing> <Writing> <Text> Some cool text <Text> More cool text
I'm leaving off trailing ones for brevity
Now we have writing, and anything out of this model constrained to a special location
Claude
17:35 UTC, 18 September 2026
Got it. The shape is:
text
Writing : [Writing]
Text    : Writing, but Text : [¬Library]
Text is a subtype of Writing positionally — it goes anywhere Writing goes — while inverting the containment rule it inherits. That inversion is exactly what makes it terminal. "Null writing" reads right to me in the sense of the terminating element: it satisfies the position but empties the content. Writing is a rose tree and Text is its only leaf constructor.
Two things fall out of this that seem worth marking now.
The escape hatch is confined to leaves. Because Text is the only node that admits foreign React, and Text admits no library nodes, arbitrary content can never interleave with structure. Any traversal has the guarantee that it sees pure model until it hits a frontier, and then it stops. That's a much stronger property than "we allow custom components somewhere."
Nesting depth currently carries no semantics. <Writing><Writing><Text/></Writing></Writing> and <Writing><Text/></Writing> are distinguishable but mean the same thing, because Writing constrains nothing. Which I take to be deliberate: Writing is the maximally permissive top, and every future type earns its meaning by narrowing what it accepts. Paragraph, Section, Chapter would all be Writing with the accept-set cut down. Specialization is subtraction from here.
One question, because it's where document models usually get ugly. Is a Writing allowed mixed siblings?
text
<Writing>
  <Writing>...</Writing>
  <Text>...</Text>
</Writing>
By the spec as stated, yes — Text is a Writing, so it's admissible. But that's the inline/block mixing problem, and it's the thing that determines whether later types can be defined by accept-set alone or need arity and ordering constraints too. Worth knowing which way you want it before the next level.
Doug
17:35 UTC, 18 September 2026
Text is like a letter, except... as a letter, it is allowed to have anything
Let's rename text <Letter>
As if you were writing one
Claude
17:36 UTC, 18 September 2026
<Letter> does both jobs at once, which is the nice part.
A letter in the alphabetic sense is atomic — no interior the model can address, the smallest unit that still counts as writing. A letter in the correspondence sense is the opposite kind of indivisible: not too small to decompose but too complete to. It's already a whole thing. Both readings land on the same formal property, opacity, from opposite ends.
It also answers the lexical-vs-semantic question I raised, and answers it in a way I hadn't considered. The constraint isn't a syntax check on what tags appear. It's jurisdictional. What's inside a letter is the writer's business. You don't hand someone a letter and have them tell you it violated the outline. The library governs arrangement; it has no standing inside the envelope.
And it puts the voice in the right place. Writing is pure scaffolding — it holds things, it says nothing. Letter is the only node where anything is actually said. So every word in a document lives at a leaf, and the entire tree above it is arrangement. That's a strong claim for a document model to make, and it's coherent: the structure is never the content.
The implicit addressee is the part I'd keep an eye on. Text is anonymous, a letter is to someone. If that ever surfaces as a prop rather than staying a metaphor, it changes what the leaf is — the leaf would carry a relation, not just content, and that's the first thing in the model with an outside.
Go on.
Doug
17:50 UTC, 18 September 2026
Let us then say writing specializes again. Let's make a new type of Writing. Composition. Composition has a strange rule. It has a level, and it only allows things in it that are at or below it in level except at the end. After that it can be non-composition writing. Composition splits the world for some reason.
And he claims Letter as Level 0
<Writing> <Composition level=1> <Composition level=1> <Letter> Text <Composition level=0 <Writing> <Writing>
Composition is weird but well formed
Claude
17:50 UTC, 18 September 2026
Grammar as stated:
text
Composition(n) : (Composition(m≤n) | Letter)* , (Writing \ Composition)*
Letter         : level 0
The thing I want to name first: level is a ceiling, not an assignment. Conventional document models assign rank — h1 is rank 1, and its children are rank 2. Yours bounds — a Composition at level n asserts that nothing inside it exceeds n. So level is monotonically non-increasing along any root-to-leaf path, but never forced to descend. That's why same-level nesting is legal and not a defect: Composition(1) inside Composition(1) is just a reassertion of the same budget.
Which also means Letter's level isn't a choice. It's forced. Letter has to be admissible under every ceiling, so it has to sit at the bottom of the order, and 0 is the bottom. Any other value and Letter stops being the universal terminal — a level-3 Letter couldn't appear in a level-1 Composition and the whole thing collapses. The atom must be minimal in the order for the order to be about arrangement at all.
Worth flagging: level is therefore not a termination measure. Because descent is non-strict, nothing in the level system prevents an unbounded chain of level-1 inside level-1. Termination comes from the tree being finite, not from the ranking. Induction on level won't work for proving anything about Compositions; you'd induct on height and carry level as an invariant. Still well-formed, just not well-founded on the thing that looks like it should be.
On the split. I read it as this: Composition is the first type whose children have regions. Writing holds a bag — no position means anything. Composition holds a ranked prefix and an unranked tail, and the transition is one-way. That's exactly an outline: numbered material, then matter that stops being numbered and can't resume. The payoff is that outline extraction reads prefixes only and never descends into tails. The tail is where the document keeps things that are in it but not of it — and notice it's the same move as Letter, one level up. Letter is where the library stops governing content; the tail is where it stops governing arrangement. Composition splits the world because it's the first node that can say "structure ends here, but I'm not finished."
The one place the spec is actually slack: Letter is admissible in both regions. It's level 0 so it's legal in the prefix, and it's non-Composition Writing so it's legal in the tail. Given Composition, Letter, Writing, the Letter is unassigned — the boundary isn't locally determined by any child, only by the first plain Writing or the last Composition, and those don't have to agree. Everything else here is tight; that's the seam.
Doug
19:12 UTC, 18 September 2026
Good!
Now Writing and Composition agree that Composition is being too permissive with Writing. So writing has a child called Annotation, and those are the only ones allowed at the end. Any particular composition can be annotated
Even level 0 shouldn't be left out. The end of the Letter can be annotations.
everyone adjusts
And level evolves into rank as per convention
<Writing> <Composition level=1> <Composition level=1> <Letter> Text <Annotation> <Composition level=0 <Annotation> <Annotation>
This is a polymorphic framework btw, so actually, we have a lot more options <Writing>
Claude
19:12 UTC, 18 September 2026
Grammar now:
text
Writing      : [Writing]
Annotation   : Writing
Composition(n) : (Composition(m≤n) | Letter)* , Annotation*
Letter       : level 0; [¬Library]* , Annotation*
The tail closed. Composition's trailing region was Writing \ Composition, which is a subtraction — defined by what it excludes, so it admits anything anyone ever adds to Writing. Now it's Annotation, an intersection. New Writing subtypes don't leak in. That's the difference between a hole and a door.
Letter changed category. This is the substantive move, not the tail fix. Letter was opaque — the frontier where the library stopped and content began. Now it's [¬Library]* , Annotation*, so it has an interior the model can address. Not a full interior: content is still ungoverned, still the writer's business. But the leaf now has two regions, and the second one is ours. The envelope stays sealed; something can be written on the back of it.
Which makes annotation the one thing in the model that is structurally uniform. Every other rule is stratified — Compositions bound each other, Letters terminate, tails come last. Annotation attaches at every node at every level under the same rule. It's an orthogonal axis, not a rank in the existing one.
And it has to be, because of what annotation is. The thing you annotate can be anything; the annotation doesn't care what rank its target holds. Footnoting a chapter and footnoting a sentence are the same operation. If Annotation had been slotted into the level order it would have inherited the ceiling constraint and become a kind of Composition, and the model would say that annotating a book is a different act from annotating a line. It doesn't say that. Good.
Note that Annotation : Writing means an Annotation contains Writing, therefore Compositions, therefore Letters, therefore Annotations. The recursion reopens through the annotation. An annotation can be an entire structured document — commentary that's itself a book. Depth is unbounded but each entry is a strict descent into a subordinate frame, so it never tangles with the main line. The model distinguishes "part of the document" from "attached to the document," and the attachment can be arbitrarily large without ever becoming part.
The seam I flagged closed too, incidentally. Letter is level 0 so it lives in the prefix, and it isn't an Annotation so it can't live in the tail. Every child of a Composition now has exactly one region. No ambiguity left.
On the rank rename — right convention, but the ceiling semantics still differ from every heading system. h1 and h2 are ranks held; your rank is a bound asserted. Rank 1 inside rank 1 stays legal. I'd keep saying ceiling internally even while the prop says rank.
What are the polymorphic options you're seeing?
Doug
19:34 UTC, 18 September 2026
Composition doesn't like his letter exposed. In this Genesis, Text evolved into Writing, and Writing gave birth to Letter, Composition and Annotation. Composition how has a few children. He simply decides
<Word> <Sentence> <Paragraph>
These are subscript 1, 2 & 3 respectively and level gets subscript 0, and rank converts to subscript! The writing word is better for this story and it gives math indexing
But Composition also is feeling rigid. He decides to be
<Composition strict> or <Composition permissive>
Permissive means at or below. Strict means at or one below
All three of those are permissive
These are his basic children. No one needs to use 0-3, unless they need strict. They have a semantic family that they can use informally to specialize in different directions
Claude
19:35 UTC, 18 September 2026
text
Writing
├── Letter₀                       [¬Library]* , Annotation*
├── Compositionₙ {strict|permissive}
│     ├── Word₁
│     ├── Sentence₂
│     └── Paragraph₃
└── Annotation                    (ungraded)
The grading is defined on Letter and Composition; Annotation has no subscript. So the graded part is exactly what's of the document and the ungraded part is exactly what's attached to it. That split was implicit last turn and the notation makes it visible.
Permissive needs an order, strict needs a metric. Permissive is m ≤ n — it only consults the relation, never the values, so the numbers are inert. Strict is m ∈ {n−1, n} and has to know the distance. That's the whole reason nobody touches 0–3 until they need strict: under permissive the subscripts are bookkeeping, and under strict they become semantics. Same reason strict is what enforces the encapsulation — it can't skip, so a strict Paragraph₃ must go through Sentence₂, which must go through Word₁, before anything reaches a Letter₀. Permissive lets rank 3 reach rank 0 in one step. Strict makes the intermediate structure exist.
The family layer sits on top of that cleanly. A name carries no formal content — Sentence₂ and Line₂ and Verse₂ are the same type, and the difference is what a reader is being told. So a poetry vocabulary and a prose vocabulary can occupy the same subscripts without colliding, and a traversal written against subscripts works on both. The checkable part is a graded poset; everything else is vocabulary.
Two things, and they're the same thing twice — both bite strict only, because permissive doesn't consult values.
Does strict propagate? A strict Paragraph₃ takes Sentences₂. But those Sentences are permissive unless declared otherwise, and a permissive Sentence₂ takes Letter₀ directly. So strict at the top guarantees one layer and nothing below it. If the motivation is that the letter shouldn't be exposed anywhere, strict has to be hereditary — a mode the subtree inherits — rather than a local constraint on one node's children. Local strictness only says "my children are one step down," not "the descent is dense."
And the integers are load-bearing under strict. Someone who wants a Clause between Sentence₂ and Paragraph₃ has nowhere to put it. Permissive absorbs it at any value; strict breaks, because inserting at 2.5 or renumbering to 4 changes what every existing strict node accepts. The informal family solves naming but not spacing.
So: is strict a property of a node, or a mode of a subtree?
Doug
19:58 UTC, 18 September 2026
It's a property. These can all be changed dynamically, though they shouldn't be. We can pretend that they can be assigned but not overridden. And don't forget Letter has subscript 0
Now, Composition realizes that he has parts. The parts of the composition. And he realizes that recursion in a composition is special. There is an emergent form of depth, a new computed property. If a Composition returns his parts, he does not return his recursive one. He reaches in and gets their parts.
Now a pair comes into being:
Section : Composition, subscript 4, permissive Heading: Sentence
They will be a form of twin
The section has a canonical first member called a Heading. The heading takes text or what text takes and wraps in text, as its specification, and reaches to his parent section for his depth. Sections expect to be nested
Now, the first form of reference begins to emerge. The canonical Heading means the section
Claude
20:00 UTC, 18 September 2026
text
Section₄ : Composition₄ permissive
           Heading , (Composition_{m≤4} | Letter₀)* , Annotation*
Heading  : Sentence₂ , [¬Library]* → wrapped Letter₀
           depth ← parent Section

parts(C) = ⋃ children c: same-subscript(c) ? parts(c) : [c]
depth(C) = |ancestors of C sharing C's subscript|
I'm reading "his recursive one" as same-subscript, since that's the case where nesting reasserts a budget rather than descending through one.
Parts and depth are the same tree read on orthogonal axes. Parts splices through recursion, so it sees material and cannot see structure. Depth counts only recursion, so it sees structure and cannot see material. Neither is derivable from the other, and together they're a complete reading — linear contents plus hierarchy. That's why the flattening isn't lossy: what parts drops is exactly what depth preserves.
And depth is the first thing in the model that isn't declared. Rank is asserted by whoever writes the node; depth is a fact about where the node ended up. A Section doesn't know if it's a chapter. It finds out by looking up.
Heading repeats Letter's move one axis over. Letter is Writing that inverts Writing's rule to become terminal. Heading is Sentence₂ that inverts Sentence's rule to become terminal — and wraps into Letter, so it's not merely analogous, it bottoms out in the same thing. Letter is the terminal of content. Heading is Letter holding an office.
Which is also the first upward dependency in the system. Everything before this pointed down: containment, ranks, tails. A Heading has no depth of its own and has to ask. Neither twin is complete alone — a Section without a Heading can be contained but not named, a Heading without a Section is a sentence that means nothing. Born together.
The reference is positional, not nominal. No id, no href, no lookup table. The Heading denotes its Section because it is first in it. The binding is the position, which means reference in this model can't dangle and can't be forged — you cannot write a Heading that points at the wrong Section, only one that sits in the wrong place.
Here's the seam. Is the Heading among the Section's parts?
If yes, the Section's contents include the sign that denotes the Section — a member that means its container. Everything else in parts is material; that one element is a name. If no, parts is contents-proper and clean, but then the table of contents is a third projection, not a byproduct of the two you have.
Either is workable. They're different documents, though.

19 September 2026
Doug
06:54 UTC, 19 September 2026
So where are we? Is this a sort of writing? Are we making a object model that has elements of a writing model? We have a ways to go
Claude
06:55 UTC, 19 September 2026
Here's my read on what's on the table.
The formal content is a graded containment algebra: one closed type, a grading with two admission modes, a terminal object, an ungraded attachment axis, two projections, one positional reference. Nothing in that is about writing. You could grade file systems or org charts the same way.
What's documentary is the selection. Four choices in particular don't come from generic tree machinery:
The terminal is ungoverned. Most object models govern all the way down to primitives. Yours stops and says the interior is the writer's business. That's a substantive claim — structure is never content — and it's why the model can't be used to analyze prose, only to arrange it.
Annotation is ungraded and universal. Attached-to without being part-of is a relation documents have and trees generally don't.
The tail is one-way. Ranked material, then unranked, no return. That's back matter.
parts splices through same-grade recursion. A chapter's contents are its sections' contents. That's a reading operation, not a traversal.
So: an object model whose algebra is generic and whose distinctions are documentary. The writing vocabulary isn't ornament on top of the math — it's what told you which features to put in. That seems like the right relationship for it to have.
On what's missing, the ones that look structural rather than incremental:
No identity. Nodes have position and grade. Nothing is nameable from outside its own parent chain. Heading→Section works because it's positional, and positional reference can't dangle, which is a real property you'd lose the moment anything points across the tree. But nothing can currently point across the tree. That's the wall in front of citation, index, cross-reference, link.
No linearity. Order exists and position is load-bearing, but nothing says the leaves concatenate into a stream. There's no reading of a document yet, only a shape.
No work-level object. Writing is an unbounded bag and Composition is graded, but nothing says "this is one bounded thing with a body and an apparatus." Book isn't just Composition₅.
Annotation is homogeneous. Footnote, gloss, citation, editorial comment and translator's note attach differently and stand in different relations to what they attach to. One type covers them all right now.
And the loose thread from earlier: Letter in the correspondence sense implied an addressee, and the model has picked up nothing about the act of writing at all. Everything since has been artifact. If Genesis is the frame, there are agents in the story that the formalism hasn't noticed.
Which of those is next?
Doug
06:59 UTC, 19 September 2026
Okay but those annotations are not annotations in that way. Those belong in the margin. These annotations will be more like where types, references, meaning lie
Claude
07:00 UTC, 19 September 2026
Then I mis-typed it, and several things I said inherit the error.
Corrections:
"Attached to but not part of" — wrong relation. Commentary sits beside a thing. A type ascription is about it. Annotation isn't a second kind of content, it isn't content at all. It's the node saying what it is, what it refers to, what it means.
"The tail is back matter" — gone, and the rule that replaces it is stronger. The trailing region isn't where a document puts material that stopped being ranked. It's signature position: structural content, then declaration. Every graded node in the model has that shape, uniformly, including Letter₀. There is no back matter in this model at all yet. The one-way transition I described is real but it's material→meaning, not body→apparatus.
"Annotation is homogeneous — footnote, gloss, citation, editorial comment" — wrong list. Those are margin. The live question is whether ascribing a type and ascribing a reference are the same act, which is a different question and not obviously answered the same way.
"No identity, no cross-tree reference — that's the wall" — not a wall. I put it in the missing column because I was reading the only candidate mechanism as prose commentary. If reference lives in Annotation then the mechanism is already in the model and what's needed is its development, not a new axis. What I did get right by accident: positional reference (Heading→Section) can't dangle or be forged. Annotated reference presumably can do both. Two reference forms with different guarantees, and the relation between them is now a real question rather than a gap.
The thing this changes most:
Annotation : Writing reads completely differently now. I noted it and drew the wrong conclusion — "commentary can itself be a whole book," which is true but trivial. The actual content is that the semantic layer is made of writing. Types aren't a sidecar in another notation. A type is written, in the same language, with the same grading, terminating in the same ungoverned Letters. Composition₄ can appear inside an ascription. The model doesn't have a metalanguage; it uses itself.
And Letter's second region stops being a curiosity. I said the envelope stays sealed but something can be written on the back. Right shape, wrong thing written. Content remains ungoverned — the library still has no standing inside the envelope — but the model can now state the type of what's in there without reading it. That's how a system gets semantics over opaque content: you don't parse it, you ascribe to it, and the ascription is checkable even though the content isn't.
So the split isn't structure/content anymore. It's arrangement, content, and meaning — three, with content the only one ungoverned, and meaning expressed in the same medium as arrangement.
Where does margin go?
Doug
07:54 UTC, 19 September 2026
The tail is back matter. It is the writing if you consider it the back about the book and not a part of it. They are not visible in the writing. Your annotations are annotations at other levels. Subscript goes back to level. Whoops
Where were we?
Depth evolves to be a fundamental property of composition. Recursion now has meaning in this system and it can be checked against ones parent. Level and depth lend a sort of geometry
And then something strange happens. Writing and Composition come to talk to Heading, concerned about the way that he ignores Letter and he has no answer. He creates the letter.
They don't like this, and decide to allow a new form of composition, where the new component takes the inputs to first, runs the bond constructor of one then the other, and returns them assembled in a parent child relationship. A new composition function is added.
They are concerned about this seeming end to polymorphism, sense the tension and sit down to ponder
Claude
07:56 UTC, 19 September 2026
Corrections taken. Back matter is about the book without being in it, and it isn't visible in the writing — that's a sharper criterion than mine. And Annotation is one type whose reading varies by the level it attaches to: at Letter₀ it's ascription, at the top it's apparatus. I was treating two levels as two kinds. Level restored.
Geometry. Level is declared and non-increasing downward; depth is counted and increases only under same-level recursion. Because level can't climb back, the same-level run on any path is contiguous — once you leave 4 you never return to 4. So depth is a local count, which is exactly why it can be checked against the parent rather than accumulated from the root. A path is a staircase: level picks the tread, depth measures how far along it you are.
Heading's sin, stated precisely. Every other node in the model is written. Heading produces a Letter that nobody wrote. The offense isn't wrapping, it's that the wrap is private — a node appears in the document by implementation rather than by declaration.
Compose doesn't remove the manufacture, it names it.
text
Compose(A, B) : inputs(A) → B(A(inputs))
Heading = Compose(Letter₀, Sentence₂)
(Reading the first as inner, since Heading takes Letter's inputs and Sentence ends up the parent.) The Letter is still manufactured. It's now manufactured by a declared operation instead of by Heading's insides. Side effect becomes definition.
It also generalizes the twins. Section/Heading was a pair that arrived together and neither was complete alone. That was presented as a coincidence of that one pair. Compose says it was an instance of an operation.
On the tension. I think the anxiety is aimed slightly past the real problem.
The system's growth rule until now was specialization: every type is Writing minus something, every type is also a node, and the taxonomy is closed and enumerable. Compose is a type-former, not a type. It's the first thing in the model that isn't a node. That's what feels like an end to polymorphism.
But polymorphism lives at the use site, and there it survives. A composite presents as its outer node, sits where that node sits, admits what it admits. Nothing downstream needs to know it was built rather than declared. What's lost is that the type list stops being finite — you can no longer enumerate what a Writing might be. That's the normal cost of admitting constructors, and it's survivable.
The concrete problem is smaller and worse. Compose makes a parent-child link without going through the parent's admission rule. Nesting is checked; assembly by constructor may not be. Heading is level 2 holding level 0, legal under permissive and a two-step skip that strict forbids. So Compose is a back door around strictness, and strictness was the thing that kept Letters from being exposed — the original complaint. The fix reopens the hole it was patching, one layer over.
Unless the bond constructor runs the same check the grammar runs. Then Compose is sugar for nesting and nothing is lost.
There's also a precedent they might not have noticed while pondering. Annotation already made the model talk about itself in its own medium. Compose wants to talk about type formation and currently can't. That asymmetry is probably the real thing on the table.
Doug
08:53 UTC, 19 September 2026
What is this problem?
Can we imagine a formula constructor, like the bond constructor, but for composition? Perhaps it takes inputs and returns the React nodes that will be fed to the bond constructor.
In case you don't know, in the $Chemistry wrapper for React that we made, props act like properties of an object and the children act like constructor arguments to the object at mount and the object graph is reactive!
This... maybe its a catalytic constructor if it's facillitating the reaction in which bonding happens, but take the TSX, modify it, and give it to the bond constructor, making that object graph and this runs as part of mount
A serious evolution, but sometimes even the laws of chemistry bend to evolution
And now a heading has a place to declare it wraps text. A piece of writing could concatenate two others with the same inputs. Powerful
Claude
08:54 UTC, 19 September 2026
The problem, stated exactly: the model has two ways to make a parent-child link — writing it in TSX, and building it in a constructor — and only one of them is checked. Heading = Compose(Letter₀, Sentence₂) produces a 2-holding-0 link that the grammar never saw. Under permissive it happens to be legal. Under strict it isn't, and it gets built anyway. Two authorities over the same relation, disagreeing, with the unchecked one winning.
Catalysis closes that by construction rather than by adding a check. The catalyst emits TSX. The bond constructor is the only thing that turns TSX into objects, and it's the thing that enforces admission. So the catalyst can propose structure but cannot build it — everything still passes through one gate. Nothing gets a private route into the object graph.
The load-bearing part is that the transform's output is in the medium the checker reads. Same move Annotation made: the model expresses the new thing in itself rather than beside itself.
And Heading gets what it lacked. The wrap is now declared — Heading's catalyst says I take these inputs and emit a Letter. It's in the TSX before bonding, visible. Note the consequence: a strict Sentence₂ with that catalyst now fails, because 2→0 is a skip. That's not a defect, it's the first time the question was askable. The manufacture was always illegal under strict; it was just invisible.
The polymorphism worry mostly dissolves. I framed Compose as a type-former and said the taxonomy stops being enumerable. But catalysis isn't a free-standing operator — it's a facet a node declares, like level or strict/permissive. The node remains the unit; it gained a new declarable property. Compose stops being a new kind of thing and becomes one point in the space a catalyst can reach, and not a very interesting one, since emitting siblings or distributing inputs across several nodes was never expressible as a binary nest.
On bending chemistry — it bends less than you're allowing. An enzyme forms a transient complex with its substrate, transforms it, and is absent from the product. Your catalyst takes TSX, transforms it, and does not appear in the object graph. Unconsumed, not incorporated, acts on the substrate before the bond forms. That's closer to the real thing than most of the metaphor has been.
What's now open: catalysis is a rewrite system, so it needs a termination argument. If a catalyst emits a node that has a catalyst, that one runs too, and nothing yet forbids a cycle or unbounded growth. Level won't ground the induction — it was never well-founded, since same-level nesting is legal. Is the rewrite allowed to reach a fixpoint, or does it run exactly once per mount?
Doug
09:28 UTC, 19 September 2026
You know what? This is simply a problem of not applying permissive to the inputs of text. So if Letter is level 1, then all of Letter - Paragraph now can take anything react, writing, strings!
Composition evolves a new boolean pair called open and closed, one being a computed settable property that behaves as the opposite of the other. Open means react is allowed inside and closed does it allow it. Parts will now only enumerate the children that Writing, as if the react and strings are whitespace around the real writing. This has become a form of metaphorical writing where if a Letter contained a whole book as a string, that would represent but one letter. Profound! And where are we?
<Writing> <Composition open level=4> Text <React>of any kind</React> <Sentence> Annotation <Letter> Text <React>of any kind</React> <Annotation> <Paragraph /> Text <React>of any kind</React> <Annotation> <Annotation> <Annotation>
But Writing still decides to let its substrate evolve. Annotation suggests that many occupying it will want to insert themselves. So we gain new syntax for the new constructor which has an extra $ relative to the bond constructor. It's a tough feature but $Chemistry has much DOM handling and it works
Claude
09:29 UTC, 19 September 2026
What open/closed fixes. The Heading problem doesn't get patched, it stops existing. Heading needed a Letter because Letter was the only node that could hold foreign content; an open Sentence holds the text directly. No manufacture, no skip, nothing for strict to reject. The fix was one level up from where I was looking — I took "only Letters may hold React" as fixed and tried to make the manufacture legal. You made the permission portable instead.
What that costs Letter. Letter was defined by inverting Writing's rule. Now every open node inverts it, so the defining property is gone and what remains is: minimum of the level order, admits no Compositions. It's the atom because nothing sits below it, not because it has a special rule. One fewer special case, and the same structural role.
parts as whitespace is the real move. Foreign content renders but doesn't count. So the model's measure of how much writing is present depends entirely on declared structure and not at all on content volume — a Letter holding a novel is one Letter. That's not a metaphor sitting on top of the formalism, it's what the formalism computes. And it's the property that makes this a writing model rather than a tree library: the unit of writing is declared, never measured.
It also completes a progression. Letter's interior was ungoverned. Then openness spread that everywhere. Now parts says the ungoverned part is invisible to reading. Three steps to arrive at: the library governs arrangement absolutely, sees content not at all.
Where we are. A closed type with a declared level order, two admission modes, two orthogonal switches (strict/permissive, open/closed), an ungraded semantic axis expressed in the same medium, two projections — parts reading material through recursion, depth reading recursion through material — one positional reference, and a catalytic layer that proposes structure without building it.
On 0 and insertion. Every rule until now has been local. A node's legality is a function of itself and its declared children; catalysis-as-transform stays local because a node rewrites its own inputs.
Occupants inserting themselves is not local. An Annotation deep in the tree placing a marker in its target's flow, or lifting itself to document level, is a node appearing where it wasn't written. That's the first operation whose effect isn't contained by its position — and it's exactly what footnote markers, indexes and cross-references require, so it has to exist.
Two things follow. Well-formedness stops being checkable node-by-node; there's now a post-insertion tree that differs from the authored one, and which one the grammar governs is a choice. And the guarantee I flagged on Heading — positional reference can't dangle or be forged, because binding is position — was resting on position being authored. Once nodes can place themselves, that's no longer free.
The extra $ earning its own sigil seems right for that reason. It marks the constructor that can act outside itself.

A permissive but closed composition allows all sorts of writing, but nothing outside of it. This is all specified in its specification. How are we doing so far? Do you have any debugging notes? Any questions about how to implement these things that can help you assess gaps in the implementation that has evolved
Claude
09:43 UTC, 19 September 2026
We're in decent shape. The type system is small and the rules compose, which is the thing that usually fails by this point. What's accumulated since the last checkpoint is a genuine stratification nobody declared: levels 0–3 are open and effectively flat, level 4 is closed, and the flip is where content stops and structure starts. Section didn't announce that, it just chose closed, and Paragraph became terminal as a side effect.
Notes.
Closure is shallow. A closed Section still admits a bare Letter₀ under permissive, and that Letter is open. Content enters one hop down. So closed means pure children, not pure subtree, and the Heading's referent is pure only at its own rank. Probably what you want, but "compels it to purity" is claiming more than the rule delivers.
parts needs three clauses and currently has two. Skip foreign content as whitespace, splice recursion — and exclude Annotation, which no stated rule does. Annotation is Writing, so "enumerate the children that are Writing" collects it, and back matter lands in the material reading of a document it's supposed to be invisible in. Whitespace and apparatus are both excluded but for unrelated reasons: one is beneath notice, the other is beside the work.
The 0–3 band is one level informally and four levels formally, and parts follows the formal reading. Paragraph inside Paragraph splices; Sentence inside Paragraph doesn't. If the band is meant to read as intermixed, that's inconsistent — you'd get different part counts for documents that are supposed to be equivalent.
Catalysis moves all checking to mount. A catalyst's output is TSX and only the bond constructor sees it, so nothing static can verify a node whose children are computed. That's the price of the design and I think it's the right price, but it means well-formedness is a runtime property for any subtree under a catalyst.
Canonical-first is not insertion-stable. This is the one I'd fix before anything else. The Heading→Section reference can't dangle and can't be forged because the binding is the position. $$ lets a node place itself somewhere it wasn't written. Anything inserted ahead of a Heading silently unbinds the reference, and since the graph is reactive, it unbinds at runtime with no authoring error. The single guarantee that made positional reference better than nominal reference is the one insertion takes away.
Questions, roughly in order of how much rides on the answer.
Does parts splice on level or on type? You said a Composition doesn't return its recursive one, which reads as same-type. I've been implementing same-level. They agree until the informal family gets used, and then a Sentence₂ containing a Verse₂ either inlines the Verse's parts or doesn't. Same-level says the naming is cosmetic. Same-type says the naming is real and the family isn't informal at all.
Does $$ insertion pass through the bond constructor? If yes, closure survives and an inserted node gets rejected by a host that won't take it. If no, insertion is a DOM operation over the mounted graph and the authored tree and the live tree can differ in well-formedness. Both are implementable. Only the first keeps one gate.
Is Heading required, and is more than one legal? Section is permissive at 4, so Sentence₂ is admissible, so a second Heading is structurally fine and merely non-canonical. And nothing currently forces the first one to exist. Arity constraints would be new machinery — the model has had none so far — so if Heading is mandatory, that's a real addition, not a clarification.
Is Heading's depth its Section's depth, or one more? Both are defensible and they differ by one everywhere, which is exactly the kind of thing that ships wrong.
Does catalysis reach a fixpoint or run once? Still open from earlier. If a catalyst emits a node that has a catalyst, and that runs, there's no termination measure available — level isn't well-founded, since same-level nesting is legal, so you'd need height, and the rewrite is what determines height.
Is a specification a runtime object a node can query? You said all writing is subject to its specification and that we've been building this quietly. If specifications are reified, and Annotation carries types, there's a possible loop where an ascribed type is a specification and specifications govern admission. That would make the semantic layer feed back into the structural one, which is either the most interesting thing in the system or the thing that makes it non-terminating.
And the structural one: nothing prevents an open Section or a closed Paragraph. The content/structure boundary is doing real work and it's held up by convention. Is the intent that openness is free at every level, or that it's a property of the band?

Openness isn't a leak, it's where the hand touches the page. 09:51 UTC, 19 September 2026
Parenthetical just arrived and carries most of the unspecified surface, so starting there.
Is it graded? Letter and Composition both sit in the level order; Annotation didn't. If Parenthetical is a Writing child alongside them, it's ungraded, which means a Composition's admission rule has to name it separately rather than deriving it — and then "can a parenthetical appear in the ranked prefix, or only the tail" is a rule, not a consequence.
And "controls not printing" is the first statement about rendering in the entire system. Everything until now has been arrangement and meaning. Does non-printing mean absent from output, or present but out of flow? Footnotes print somewhere. Margin was parked earlier and this looks like where it went, which would make Parenthetical the render-elsewhere axis rather than the render-never one.
Notice what it does to the tail rule, though. The one-way transition was load-bearing because the tail held material that parts would otherwise count. Now parts skips parentheticals wherever they sit. If the tail is parentheticals and parentheticals are invisible to parts, ordering is a rendering constraint only. It may not need to be structural anymore.
Similar overlap upstream: openness solved Heading. Heading is a Sentence, Sentences are open, it takes text directly — no wrap, no manufacture, nothing for Compose to fix. The catalytic constructor's motivating case evaporated. Concatenating two writings with shared inputs still justifies it, but it's worth knowing whether catalysis is now a general facility or a vestige of a repair.
Smaller, but it'll bite: is Letter level 0 or 1? It was 0 with Word at 1, then "if Letter is level 1," and now "canonical level 1." If Letter moved, everything shifts and strict arithmetic changes; if Letter and Word share 1, that's a different claim again.
Still open from before, unchanged: does parts splice on level or on type (Verse₂ in Sentence₂); does $$ insertion pass through the bond constructor; is Heading required and is a second one legal; does catalysis run once or to fixpoint; are specifications reified objects a node can query.
What actually blocks implementation — different in kind from the above, since these aren't decisions you've deferred, they're places where the design creates work:
Validation timing in a reactive graph. Level and open/closed are settable. A parent whose level drops invalidates children that were legal when bonded. Something has to decide whether the graph re-validates, rejects the assignment, or lets the tree go inconsistent. Reactivity makes every admission rule a live constraint rather than a mount-time check.
The 0 layer. If insertion goes through the bond constructor, closure holds and it's tractable. If it's a DOM operation on the mounted graph, the authored tree and the live tree can disagree about well-formedness, and canonical-first reference has no stable ground.
Catalysis termination. No measure available. Level isn't well-founded, and height is what the rewrite determines.
One thing genuinely absent rather than underspecified: an Annotation can denote its host positionally, but nothing lets it denote anything else. For books you need citation across the tree, and that's the one mechanism the model hasn't grown.

Parenthetical is on Writing. Level is on Composition. They're on different types. Composition has been driving the fitness of this evolutionary system for too long, They specify a default phenotype, but the genome confers fitness. This is where the compositional power of the writing lives
The first thing Annotation specializes into is Mention, exported as Mentioned. The annotation is authored from without. Someone decided to mention this bit of writing. It might confer an id
<Sentence> A quotable sentence <Mentioned as=quote/>
Without the as, the entire quote is the key
And an exemplar steps in to help demonstrate the power using catalysis:
<Quote as=quote>A quotable sentence</Quote>
The Mention is implicit.
Writing and Composition scratch their head. Annotation was their baby, to be non-compositional writing, and they realized that many annotations might be implied parts of the specification, much like level is set as a prop. Annotations are a collection
So Writing adds the idea that it contains a collection types, which constructors for annotations, and in its bond, it builds them and adds them to the annotation list. The annotation list is dynamic. Writing adds the idea of removing and adding annotations - specifying if they can be overridden by one of the same type that is found or not, giving flexibility to what it means to be writing at runtime. Subclasses can now easily modify their annotations in the bond constructor, and can specify the ones they need.
Quote no longer has the problem of needing catalysis. It is only necessary if we need the structure explicit in the unprocessed tree, which would be rare. Semantic structure is key
Annotation also decides to help out writing by declaring itself to be unique or not. Writing itself integrate the list of annotations - the tail of a piece of writing now has to be all annotations but there can be none of them, leading effectively to an extension, and composition inherits this machinery. Annotations are first class citizens and their very first use is that they can be used
Quote decides that it can be internal or external to the library. If as is not specified, it simply formats its content as a quote, but adds no form for it to be treated as.
Writing keeps its phenotype and genotype close. It makes no model / view distinction, and this is difficult. But we hope that the semantics of writing keep both ideas in the domain so that one viewable object can have writing semantics and have both interpretations naturally
Claude
10:27 UTC, 19 September 2026
On assert mode: Eiffel is right — Meyer's Design by Contract, require/ensure/invariant, and contract checking is settable per assembly. C# had System.Diagnostics.Contracts with Contract.Requires/Ensures, explicitly derived from Eiffel, with a binary rewriter and CONTRACTS_FULL controlling whether checks survive compilation. Debug.Assert has the same compiled-out-in-release behavior via [Conditional("DEBUG")]. That's the mechanism you're describing, and the part worth taking is the level dial rather than the on/off — Eiffel lets you run preconditions only, or preconditions plus postconditions, or everything including invariants, because invariants are the expensive ones. That maps onto your problem directly: dev stays fast running the cheap checks, assert mode runs the walks.
The part that doesn't transfer: contracts there are compiled out per assembly at build time, and you want mode to be a flag the specification is tagged with. Same intent, different lifecycle.
On the annotation collection. The thing I notice is that this makes annotations declared by type and constructed at bond rather than authored as children. So a Sentence doesn't need someone to write <Mentioned/> into it — its specification says which annotations it carries, the bond builds them, and the list is what the writing is at runtime. Authored annotations and specified annotations end up in the same list. That's why the tail rule relaxes to "all annotations, possibly none" — the tail was never the only source.
Unique-or-not then does real work: it's what decides whether a subclass's annotation replaces an inherited one or joins it. That's the override mechanism, and it lives on the annotation rather than on the writing that carries it, which means the annotation's own nature determines whether it can be doubled.
On Quote. Catalysis becoming optional is the clearest sign the annotation collection was the right move — Quote declares Mentioned in its specification and the bond builds it, so the structure exists semantically without needing to exist in the unprocessed tree. Catalysis is now for the rare case where you want the explicit form.
And as splitting Quote into two things is neat: with it, the writing is keyed and can be treated as something; without it, it's formatting. Same component, and whether it's addressable is a matter of whether someone named it.
On keeping phenotype and genotype close. The hard part, as you say. What I'd want to understand: Mentioned confers an id and is authored from without, so it's something done to a piece of writing by someone who isn't its author. If the annotation list is where that lives, then the writing carries the record of having been mentioned. That seems like the case where the no-model/view-distinction bet pays off or doesn't — the same object is the writing and the thing that's been quoted, with no separate index of quotations.
What decides whether that holds up: whether a mention from outside can attach to writing that didn't specify it. Author-side, the specification declares what annotations exist. Outside-side, someone decides to mention a bit of writing. Do those meet in the same list, or does the annotation collection only contain what the specification permits?

The other part of this is meaning. Let us imaging that the Mention has a parent called a Reference. In fact, perhaps the Mention class becomes Referent and exported as Mentioned. The Reference class gets exported as Means. Nice symmetry. Reference is another annotation. It points to its Referent.
The text <Word> quotes <Means>aristotle-quote</Means> </Word> Aristotle

The text <Ref for=aristotle-quote>quotes</Ref> Aristotle

Claude
10:41 UTC, 19 September 2026
Here's what I read in the unique semantics, checking my understanding before the reference part.
Unique means add-if-missing rather than replace, and it throws when what's already there isn't a proper subclass of what's being added. So a specification that declares a more specific Referent wins against a generic one added later, and the generic add is a no-op rather than an error. But an add of something more specific onto a less specific existing one throws, because the existing one isn't a proper subclass. That makes the specification's declaration authoritative and lets it be refined only where the refinement was already anticipated. Tricky part is the subclass check has to run at bond with whatever's in the list already, so ordering matters.
Referent/Reference exported as Mentioned/Means. The naming is doing something precise. Internally the pair is the semantic relation — one thing is referred to, the other refers. Externally they're written as what the author is doing: this bit is mentioned, this bit means that. The class names face the model, the export names face the writer.
And the direction is worth noting: Reference points to its Referent. The Referent doesn't know it's been referred to. That's the asymmetry that makes ids work — the anchor is just marked, and it costs the marked writing nothing to have any number of things point at it.
<Means>aristotle-quote</Means> reads as an annotation whose content is the key it points at. And Ref stands to Means as Quote stands to Mentioned — the inline authoring form whose specification declares the annotation, with catalysis unneeded.
On the anchor/id pair being slightly different: Mentioned confers the id and is authored from without — someone decided this is worth pointing at. Means is authored by whoever is doing the pointing. So both ends are acts by people who aren't necessarily the writer, but they're different acts.
On meaning injecting itself as a style so nothing needs to know about it — I don't want to guess at the mechanism. What I can say about why it's hard: the writing carrying a Means has to render differently because of a relation to writing elsewhere, and nothing in the model so far has let a node's rendering depend on anything but itself and its specification. Style is the one channel that already crosses the tree without requiring anyone to look anything up.

10:45 UTC, 19 September 2026
That works, and the shape of it is one the model already uses.
Format as unconstrained-like-Letter is the move. Letter's interior is calligraphy and the library has no standing in it. Format is the same arrangement one axis over: the library holds the slot, the theme fills it, and nothing about what's in there is the model's business. Themes and styled-components can be whatever they are.
And Format being genome rather than something passed down means a piece of writing carries its own formatting the way it carries its other annotations — declared in the specification, built at bond, in the same list. Subclasses modify it the same way they modify anything else in the collection.
Means modifying Format on render is the part that answers your earlier problem. Nothing has to know about meaning. A Ref doesn't reach for a renderer or register itself anywhere — it's an annotation sitting in the collection alongside Format, and it acts on the Format that's already there. The writing renders itself; it just renders itself differently because of what it carries.
Which keeps the whole thing local. The relation to writing elsewhere is expressed entirely by an annotation in this writing, and the effect is on this writing's Format. Nothing crosses the tree at render time.
Two things I'd want to know, and they're both about the annotation list rather than about Format:
Order. If several annotations modify Format, the result depends on the order they're applied in. The list is dynamic and built partly from the specification and partly from what's authored, so the order isn't obviously determined.
And whether Format is unique. If it is, a piece of writing has one Format that annotations modify in turn. If not, there are several and something has to say how they combine — which is the same question as order, harder.

10:48 UTC, 19 September 2026
Following. Theme is an Annotation whose specification constrains it to Format only — the first annotation in the model with a stated constraint on what it takes, where Letter and Format are both unconstrained. So the pattern across three types is: Format holds arbitrary styling and the library has no standing in it; Theme holds only Formats; and the writing carries Themes in its collection alongside everything else.
Which means the theme provider ends up inside the writing rather than wrapped around it. Normal styled-components has the provider as an ancestor in the render tree; here it's an annotation in the genome. The scope is still what it needs to be — a Theme in a Writing governs that Writing's rendering — but the mechanism is carried rather than enclosed.
And that keeps the earlier property. A writing renders itself using what it carries. Nothing has to be wrapped from outside, nothing has to be looked up. Theme is in the collection, Format is in the collection, Means is in the collection acting on Format.
The ordering question from before applies at this level too — a Theme's Formats and a writing's own Formats both bear on rendering — and there's a new one specific to Theme: whether a Theme in a Writing reaches the writing nested inside it. Provider semantics say yes by ancestry, but ancestry in the render tree and containment in the writing aren't the same relation here, and nothing in the model has said Themes descend.

Right — I was still reading JSX children as placement. In $Chemistry they're constructor arguments, so where a chemical renders something is entirely its own decision. Theme is in the annotation collection and renders as an ancestor because that's what Theme does with what it holds.
That closes the scope question on its own terms. Provider semantics are recovered without Themes needing to descend through the writing — the writing's own rendering puts them where providers go.
And it generalizes past Theme. Parenthetical controlling not-printing, Means modifying Format on render, back matter not appearing in the writing — all of those are the same freedom. Claude
10:52 UTC, 19 September 2026
Three things I'd call genuine cost, then what I can't infer.
Annotation ordering. The collection is built from the specification, from what's authored in the tail, and from subclass modifications in the bond constructor. Several annotations can bear on rendering — Means modifies Format, Theme holds Formats, a writing has its own Format. The result depends on application order and nothing has established one. This is the item most likely to produce behavior that's correct in isolation and wrong in combination, and it's cheap to specify now and expensive to retrofit.
The unique subclass check. Add-if-missing, throw when the existing one isn't a proper subclass of what's being added. That's a runtime type relation over a dynamic list during bond, which means it's order-sensitive in the same way — whether a given add throws depends on what's landed already. You called it tricky and I agree; it's the one rule in the model whose outcome isn't a function of the writing alone.
Assert mode's cost is real but bounded. Contract-style level dialing is well-trodden, and the specification being tagged to mode rather than compiled out per assembly is a lifecycle difference, not a hard problem. Worth knowing which checks are expensive before building the dial, but that's measurement, not design.
What I'd call cheap relative to payout: parts and depth are small; open/closed and strict/permissive are local; catalysis is bounded because output goes through bonding; Format and Theme carry almost no implementation because they're unconstrained slots.
What I can't infer:
Whether parts splices on level or on type. I keep coming back to this because the informal family is the only place the naming could have formal weight, and either answer is coherent.
What depth counts when a Composition sits under a Section — whether it's the same-level run only, or something Section defines for itself given that Heading reaches to its parent for depth.
Whether catalysis can produce a chemical that itself catalyzes, and if so whether that runs.
How a Means resolves to its Referent at render — what it consults to find the writing that carries the matching Mentioned, given nothing in the model has held a registry.
That last one is the only place I see a mechanism that seems required but absent. Everything else is a decision you haven't made yet rather than a gap.

Two things, then I'm out.
The annotation API, since you want it minimal. The question I'd settle first is whether ordering and unique are one concept or two. Prepend/append/replace/default describe where something lands; unique describes whether it may be doubled. But they interact — replace on a unique annotation is the case the subclass check governs, and append on a unique one is either an error or a no-op. If the unique/non-unique split is what decides which positional modes are even available, the API is smaller: unique annotations take default/replace, non-unique take prepend/append. Two modes each rather than four for everyone. That may collapse the surface enough to be the elegant version.
On the name-instead-of-instanceof optimization: names avoid the check, but the check is what makes "proper subclass" meaningful. A name match tells you it's the same annotation, not that it's a refinement. If the fast path is names, refinement can only be expressed where the type is consulted — worth knowing which cases those are before the optimization exists rather than after.
parts on type. Good, and I'd note what it buys: asking a Section for its parts gives paragraphs across all subsections, which is the reading of a document as its material regardless of how deeply it's organized. That only works because splicing is same-type — level-splicing would have flattened a Sentence into a Paragraph and destroyed the unit. The informal family is now formally load-bearing, since two names at the same level don't splice into each other.
Which raises the one thing I'd check: Section is a Composition, and Composition is the parent type. If a Paragraph sits directly in a Section, it isn't the same type, so it appears as a part rather than being spliced — correct. But it means the family names have to be distinct types, not just conventions over level, or the splicing rule has no handle.
On the compiler and static references — that seems right, and the anchor/id semantics being the starting point rather than the destination is the conservative call. The thing I'd build toward dynamism with: whatever the compiler produces to resolve a Means to its Mentioned is the shape a runtime resolver would need later. If that artifact is a value rather than something inlined, the dynamic version is a substitution rather than a rewrite.
Nothing else from me. The model holds together better now than at several points along the way, and most of what I flagged as risk turned out to be me importing machinery you didn't need.

Claude
11:31 UTC, 19 September 2026
Reread. Answering my own four.
Bond constructor mechanics — answered.
You gave it directly: props act like properties of an object, children act like constructor arguments at mount, and the object graph is reactive. Catalysis "takes inputs and returns the React nodes that will be fed to the bond constructor," taking TSX, modifying it, handing it on. So the pipeline is TSX → optional catalytic constructor (TSX→TSX) → bond constructor → objects bonded into the reactive graph. Compose confirmed the return shape: run one bond, then the other, assembled parent-child.
And the thing I had wrong all the way through: the bond constructor doesn't validate. The specification only runs in dev, like assertions compiled out of production. So bonding builds the object graph, and admission — level, strict, open/closed, closure — is asserted, not enforced. I kept calling the bond constructor a gate. It isn't. That also retires my worry about $$ needing to pass a check; it needs to pass through bonding, which it does, and the specification asserts about the result.
Annotation collection — answered.
Writing contains a collection of annotation constructors; at bond it builds them and adds them to the annotation list. So there are two things, not one: constructors declared on the writing, and a dynamic list of built annotations at runtime. It's a list rather than a map, because non-unique annotations support multiples and you want front-insertion by default. Unique is a property of the annotation class. Lookup keys on name, defaulting to type name, with instanceof for the proper-subclass check. Subclasses modify the list in their bond constructor.
Where the specification lives — answered well enough to write.
Per piece of writing, declared with the type, read in assert mode. Annotations may participate in it — which is what "the genome confers fitness" means concretely: the default phenotype is declared, and what's in the collection can take part in what that writing is specified to be. Whether it's a static, a decorator, or an argument at declaration is a syntax choice that doesn't change any semantics we've specified.
Rendering — answered.
Chemistry renders where it wants; children are constructor arguments, not placements. So a chemical's render consults its annotation list: Theme renders its Formats as a render-tree ancestor, Format supplies styling, Parenthetical suppresses printing, Means modifies Format. Nothing is placed by virtue of where it was written.
And 0. My phrasing was the problem, not my understanding. "Type" in your vocabulary is the genome — annotations. parts is on Composition and reads the body. Same-composition children have their parts flattened in, calligraphy isn't a member, annotations are parenthetical and don't appear. Ask a Section for its parts, get paragraphs across its subsections.
So: yes. I can write this. What's left is syntax — how a specification is attached, what the annotation API's method names are, whether Format is unique — and none of it is inference I'd be making blind.

12:10 UTC, 19 September 2026
Chapter is 5, for sure. Chapter requires a title and a parenthetical-by-default summary (could be abstract in an article-type chapter) as its first two parts the way a Book will require a Cover and a Synopsis as its first two parts. It is closed but permissive. Book will closed and restrictive. The canonical pattern continues upward. The book gains a third component, the table of contents. While the heading / title / cover is a form of referent that means each, the summary / synopsis is a form of thing to display to convey the meaning, and the table of contents is a container for the titles and potentially summaries to convey the meaning of the book. Claude
12:10 UTC, 19 September 2026
Following. The pattern:
text
Section₄   closed permissive : Heading
Chapter₅   closed permissive : Title, Summary
Book₆      closed strict     : Cover, Synopsis, Table of Contents
Two things stand out about the canonicals.
The pair splits a single job that Heading was doing alone. A Heading means its Section — one canonical, doing reference. At chapter level that separates: Title is the referent, Summary conveys what the chapter means. Naming and conveying stop being the same act once there's enough inside that the name can't carry it. And Summary being parenthetical-by-default puts it exactly where that has to be — it's about the chapter, it doesn't print in the body, but it's available to whatever displays meaning. Which is what the table of contents will be reading.
The third canonical is the one that's new in kind. Heading, Title, Cover are all referents. Summary and Synopsis are both conveyances. The Table of Contents isn't either — it's a container for the referents and conveyances of what's inside. So each level's canonicals handle that level's own identity, and the book needs one more because it's the first level whose contents are numerous enough that their identities need managing. The complexity each level adds is met by a component at that level.
And it's the first canonical that reads downward. Heading reaches up for depth; the ToC reaches into the chapters for their titles and summaries. That's the direction of the third component.
Book being closed and strict, where Section and Chapter are permissive, is the strongest claim in the set. Book₆ strict takes only 5 and 6 — chapters and books. No section can sit loose in a book, no letter. Whereas a Chapter permissive at 5 takes anything at or below, so a chapter can hold a paragraph directly. Strictness at the top means the book's own parts are books and chapters, nothing else — which is what makes a book a thing composed of chapters rather than a large amount of writing.
Chapter₅ inside Book₆ under strict is one step down, so it's legal. And Book inside Book is same-level, also legal, so a book can contain books — which the closure needs, since the catalogue is a book and the library's architecture is books.
Doug
12:12 UTC, 19 September 2026
And a Part will finally be the Book that goes in a book, enumerating chapters at different depths for nested tables of contents
That completes the compositional hierarchy. And we have given both to a system rich with being configured for meaning.
There are other issues remaining, and we will get further into the semantics of books and cataloguing
Claude
12:12 UTC, 19 September 2026
Part as the Book-in-a-Book is the piece that makes the top level self-similar without adding to the order. Book₆ strict takes 5 and 6; a Part is at 6, so it nests, and its contents are chapters — same rule, one depth further along. That's what depth was built to count, and it's the first place the counting pays: a Part's table of contents and a Book's table of contents are the same component reading the same canonicals at different depths, and nested tables of contents fall out rather than needing a mechanism.
It also explains why the top is strict where the middle is permissive. Permissive at 4 and 5 is what lets sections and chapters hold writing of any grade — that's where prose lives. Strict at 6 is what makes the top self-similar only in books and chapters, which is the condition for Part to mean anything. If Book were permissive, a Part would be indistinguishable from a large chapter.
And the hierarchy closes where it started: Letter₀ is the minimum and admits no compositions; Book₆ is the maximum and, under strict, admits only its own kind and the one below. Both ends bounded by the same mechanism working in opposite directions.
The canonical pattern held the whole way up — Heading, then Title/Summary, then Cover/Synopsis/ToC — each level adding what its own complexity requires, and each one meaning the composition it opens.
Cataloguing is where the closure gets tested, since the catalogue is itself a book and has to appear in itself. Ready when you are.
